## 2026-09-19 15:44 UTC

**Idea name:** Footing Combiner — proximity-detect isolated column footings, auto-generate the merged pad outline, and close the exact gap `Convert Slab` and RC Automation's own footing module both name but neither fills

**Potential:**
A structural revision that pulls two or three columns closer together — a grid tightened for a lift core, an added column for a re-analysed span, a foundation redesign after a soil report — routinely leaves their isolated footings overlapping or too close for individual pads to satisfy edge-distance/clear-spacing rules, and the fix is always the same manual sequence: spot the clash by eye in plan, hand-measure the combined footprint with clearances around both pedestal zones, delete the isolated pads, and either sketch a new combined footing from scratch or type its outline into a schedule by hand — a 20–40 minute detour every time it happens, repeated per cluster, with no model-side warning that two footings are even close enough to need it. This repo has already named the gap twice without closing it. First, `ConvertFloor.pushbutton` — the tool this repo already built and shipped for exactly this class of "recreate on a category boundary" problem — states its own boundary explicitly: `ConvertFloor.pushbutton/README.md:84-86`, *"Isolated footings and wall foundations — those are `FamilyInstance` / `WallFoundation`, not sketch-based slabs, and are out of scope,"* echoed verbatim at runtime in `ConvertFloor.pushbutton/script.py:1470-1471`. Second, and more tellingly, RC Automation's own footing-creation module already anticipated the *shape* of this exact feature in its own design rationale and then never built it: `anongee_toolkit/structural/footings.py:1-24`'s module docstring reads *"Footings are floors here rather than family instances... A combined footing, a cut corner, a pad worked around a pile cap — all of them are just a different list of points,"* and `anongee_toolkit/rc_automation/models.py:450-462`'s `FootingPlacement.outline` field repeats the same framing — *"a combined footing... can be scheduled instead of approximated."* Both statements describe a combined footing as trivial once you have the right outline, but the "right outline" is entirely manual today: a modeler has to work out the merged polygon by hand and type it into the Excel schedule's outline column (parsed as-is by `anongee_toolkit/rc_automation/excel_engine.py:135-147`'s `parse_outline()`), with nothing in the repo ever detecting that two footings *should* be combined or computing that outline for them. On the market, automatic footing-combination (detect clash/near-clash, propose a merged pad, resize for code clearances) exists only as a feature bundled inside paid, discipline-specific foundation-design software (Tekla Tedds/Structural Designer, RISAFoundation, SAFE-linked Revit add-ins), licensed as engineering calculation suites rather than a Revit-native modelling shortcut — nothing free reads a Revit model's own isolated footings, flags the ones sitting too close, and offers a one-click merged pad.

**Description:**
`Footing Combiner.pushbutton` (sibling to `ConvertFloor.pushbutton` in `StrucBIM.stack`, and a natural second consumer of RC Automation's footing module) runs in two directions — **Combine** and **Split** — on a pre-selection or a whole-model/whole-view scan:

1. **Detect** — collect every `OST_StructuralFoundation` element, both flavours the repo already has to deal with: sketch-based `Floor`s created by RC Automation's `footings.py:316-343` (`create()`), and traditional `FamilyInstance` isolated-footing families placed by hand or by other tools (`cad2bim/builders/footings.py`, `cad2bim/footing_plan.py`, which only ever place single pads). For each pair, take their plan-footprint bounding boxes — `anongee_toolkit/structural/rebar_hosts.py:213-227`'s `plan_origin_mm()` already gives a footing's plan centre — and run the exact real-geometry-intersection query `JoinPriority.pushbutton/script.py:256-295`'s `_detect_overlaps()` already uses (`BoundingBoxIntersectsFilter` → `ElementIntersectsElementFilter`), generalized with a configurable clear-spacing buffer instead of requiring actual overlap, so two pads a hand's width apart are caught before they're touching, not after.
2. **Preview a cluster per group** — every hit becomes a row: the columns each footing carries (via `rebar_hosts.py`'s existing `index_by_key()` pattern), the isolated pad dimensions, the computed merged outline (convex hull of both footprints, expanded by a configurable edge-clearance-from-pedestal margin per column, then squared off to a rectangle or kept as the hull — user's choice per row), and a resulting area/depth-consistency check (mixed footing thickness in one cluster is flagged, not silently averaged).
3. **Combine** — ticked rows: build the merged `CurveLoop` and call `footings.py`'s own `curve_loop()` (`footings.py:235-263`) + `create()` (`footings.py:316-343`) exactly as RC Automation already does for a single pad, so a combined footing is created through the same code path a normal one is, not a special case; the source isolated footings' rebar (if any) is not guessed at — the tool reports "N isolated footings had reinforcement; re-run RC Automation or Rebar Cage Copier against the new combined pad" rather than attempting a merge it can't verify. A `FamilyInstance`-based source footing is first flagged "family-based — recreate as floor pad first," reusing `ConvertFloor.pushbutton`'s own recreate pipeline as a pre-step rather than duplicating it.
4. **Split** — the reverse: pick one combined footing, mark column locations within it (read from the columns it currently supports), and it proposes N isolated pad outlines sized to each column with the same clearance rule, for when a design reverts to separated foundations.
5. One `TransactionGroup`, one `Transaction` per cluster — a single failed cluster rolls back only that cluster, matching every other converter tool in this repo.

**Implementation:**
- New pushbutton in `Essential.panel/StrucBIM.stack/`, alongside `ConvertFloor.pushbutton` and `DrawFloor.pushbutton`, sharing their `bundle.yaml`/README/CHANGELOG layout.
- Proximity detection: lift `JoinPriority.pushbutton/script.py:256-295`'s `BoundingBoxIntersectsFilter`/`ElementIntersectsElementFilter` pattern into a small shared helper (it currently lives inlined in one pushbutton with no shared module, so this is the second consumer that justifies moving it into `anongee_toolkit`), parameterized with a clear-spacing buffer instead of the raw geometric-touch check `JoinPriority` needs for its own purpose.
- Outline geometry: convex hull + clearance offset is new logic — nothing in the repo computes a hull today (`AutoDIM.pushbutton` and `anongee_toolkit/revit/geometry.py` only ever read face existence or whole-solid volume, per the existing Formwork Takeoff idea's own research) — but the *placement* half needs zero new Revit-API surface, since `footings.py:create()`/`curve_loop()` already accept an arbitrary point list by design.
- Mixed-representation handling (Floor vs FamilyInstance footings) reuses `ConvertFloor.pushbutton`'s recreate pipeline as a callable pre-step rather than a second implementation of "read a family instance, place a floor pad instead."
- New `footing_combine_config.json`: clear-spacing threshold (mm, likely a multiple of footing width rather than one constant), edge-clearance-from-pedestal margin, `outline_mode: "hull" | "rectangle"`, mixed-depth policy (`block` default).
- Ships its own `CHANGELOG.md`, matching `AutoLevel.pushbutton`'s pattern for a tool likely to grow (rectangular-vs-hull refinement, strap-beam mode) after v1.

**Future update:**
- Strap-footing mode: instead of one solid merged pad, generate two isolated pads connected by a strap beam (`OST_StructuralFraming`) when the engineer's intent is eccentricity correction rather than a genuinely combined mat — a distinct, common alternative to a single combined pad that a real structural team will ask for once the basic combine case ships.
- Auto-trigger reinforcement re-planning on the new combined pad via RC Automation's own `plan_footing`/`place_footing` path once a combined-footing row in the Excel schedule exists, closing the "re-run RC Automation" manual step named above.
- Extend clash detection to footing-vs-grade-beam and footing-vs-existing-utility (from a linked MEP/civil model) once the column-to-column case is proven, reusing the same `BoundingBoxIntersectsFilter` helper this idea already promotes to a shared module.
- Pile-cap variant: same hull-and-clearance logic, different depth/rebar rules, for teams on piled rather than pad foundations — the `footings.py` docstring's own "a pad worked around a pile cap" line is the precedent for treating this as the same underlying geometry problem.

## 2026-09-19 10:41 UTC

**Idea name:** Family Health Auditor — in-place families, embedded-CAD geometry nested inside loadable families, and duplicate-family definitions: the three biggest causes of Revit file bloat, none of which Purge Unused (or anything in this repo) ever looks for

**Potential:**
Every mature project file eventually balloons to multiple gigabytes, and a modeler's only native recourse — Manage → Purge Unused — only ever removes *zero-instance* types, so it is structurally blind to the three things that actually cause most of the bloat: an **in-place family** (modeled directly in the project rather than as a loadable family), a **loadable family with CAD geometry imported inside it** (an "import symbol" nested in the .rfa — a single detail component or generic model authored by tracing a DWG instead of drawing native geometry, which is a well-documented, very common way a family balloons from tens of KB to tens of MB), and **two differently-named families that are actually the same geometry** (a consultant's column family re-imported under a different name than the firm's own, or a family duplicated-and-renamed by mistake) — all three are fully *in use*, so Purge Unused cannot see any of them, and Revit's own UI has no report that lists any of the three at all. A repo-wide grep for `IsInPlace`/`Family.IsInPlace` returns zero hits anywhere in `AnonGee.tab` or `anongee_toolkit` — nothing has ever read whether a family in this model is in-place. `ImportInstance` is read in exactly two places, and neither opens a family document: `cad2bim/readers/cad_links.py:16-23`'s `_collect()` finds linked/embedded CAD at the *project* level only (an `ImportInstance` with `IsLinked == False` sitting directly in the model), and `AutoLevel.pushbutton/anongee_autolevel/revit_ops.py:246` collects project-level `ImportInstance`s purely to parse level-marker text off them — neither has ever called `Document.EditFamily()` to look *inside* a loadable family for geometry nested there. The already-logged **Type Consolidator** (2026-09-13) comes closest and is explicitly a different layer: its `cs_signature()`-based grouping (lifted from `ConvertFloor.pushbutton/script.py:704-759`) finds duplicate *types within* one category by compound-structure/parameter signature — it never opens a family document, never compares two differently-named *Family* elements' actual geometry, and has nothing to say about an in-place family, which has no loadable "family" definition to group in the first place. `anongee_toolkit.structural.inventory._families()` (`inventory.py:117-134`) already walks every `ElementType` in the model for the template-audit report `Extract Template.pushbutton` produces, grouped by category/family/type/parameters — but purely as descriptive text for a human to read; it carries no `is_in_place` flag, no embedded-geometry flag, no instance count, no size or duplicate signal of any kind. On the market this exact report — in-place family census, embedded-CAD-in-family detection, cross-family duplicate detection — is sold only as one line item inside large paid whole-model housekeeping suites (Ideate Sidekick's "Model Health"/"Family Health" report, CTC BIM Tools' Health Check), both licensed per seat; nothing free produces it, and Revit's own Purge Unused is, by its own definition, incapable of ever finding any of the three.

**Description:**
`Family Health Auditor.pushbutton` (new tool in `Essential.panel/Bulk Tool.stack`, sibling to `Bulk Delete.pulldown` / `Bulk Rename.pulldown` / `Rebar.pulldown` and the already-logged Type Consolidator / Workset Assigner — the stack that already owns "scan the whole model, report, then act" housekeeping tools) runs a single read-only scan, then a scoped-action pass:

1. **Census** — starts from the same family enumeration `structural.inventory._families()` already builds (`inventory.py:117-134`), extended with the fields that report never needed: instance count per family (not just per type), whether the family `IsInPlace`, and — for loadable families only — whether a temporary edit of the family document finds any `ImportInstance` nested inside it (embedded CAD geometry).
2. **Cluster duplicates** — every non-in-place family reduced to a geometry-and-parameter signature (bounding box per type + a hash of each type's writable parameter values, the same "signature then cluster" shape `Type Consolidator` already proposes at the type level, one level up here at the family level, across family *names* instead of within one family's own types); families whose every type matches another family's signature are grouped as merge candidates.
3. **Preview report** — one row per finding, three sections: **In-place families** (name, category, instance count — report-only, no safe auto-fix); **Embedded CAD in loadable families** (family name, how many types affected — report-only); **Duplicate family clusters** (which families share a signature, instance count per family, which one the report proposes as canonical by instance count) plus a fourth, safely-actionable section: **Fully unused families** (every type has zero instances — a whole-family line Purge Unused never reports as such, even though it would eventually remove each zero-instance type piecemeal).
4. **Report-only / Act toggle** — the same two-mode switch every pre-flight tool in the repo already offers; ticking rows in the "fully unused families" section and pressing Act purges those families outright (`doc.Delete(family.Id)`), the one row type safe to automate in v1 — every other section stays report-only, named in-window as "flagged for manual review" rather than silently promising an automatic fix for a case (relinking an in-place family, merging two live families) that is materially riskier than swapping a type.
5. **Result** — counts per section, exportable through the same `openpyxl` path `Export Schedule.pushbutton`/`BBS Generator.pushbutton` already use, so a "model health" snapshot becomes a dated, reusable handover artifact instead of a one-time verbal warning.

**Implementation:**
- New `anongee_toolkit.revit.family_health` module — the first in the repo to read `Family.IsInPlace` (zero prior hits, confirmed above) and the first to call `Document.EditFamily(family)` to open a family into a temporary in-memory document for inspection, closed without saving immediately after each scan; wrapped per-family in try/except so one corrupt or workshared-locked family reports "could not open" instead of aborting the whole census.
- Embedded-CAD detection inside the temporary family document reuses the exact `ImportInstance` collector pattern `cad2bim/readers/cad_links.py:16-23` already proves at the project level (`FilteredElementCollector(doc).OfClass(ImportInstance)`), pointed at the family document instead of the project document — no new Revit API surface, only a new document to point the same call at.
- Family enumeration starts from `structural.inventory._families()` (`inventory.py:117-134`) rather than re-walking `FilteredElementCollector(doc).OfClass(ElementType)` a second time; the new health fields are appended to each row instead of duplicating the walk.
- Duplicate-family signature reuses the geometry-bbox-plus-parameter-hash approach, generalizing `ConvertFloor.pushbutton/script.py:704-716`'s `cs_signature()` for compound-structure families and adding a bounding-box+parameter-value fallback for loadable component families that have no `CompoundStructure` to read — the same category split `Type Consolidator`'s own plan already lays out for system vs. loadable families.
- Fully-unused-family purge reuses `operations/bulk_delete.py`'s `RevitTransaction`-wrapped per-item delete shape (`operations/bulk_delete.py:90,222`) rather than a new transaction pattern — a family delete that fails (still referenced somewhere the census missed) is skipped, not fatal to the batch.
- Same modeless window shell (`anongee_toolkit.ui.hostwnd`/`checklist`/`progressbar`/`theme`) every other multi-step tool in the repo already runs on; `EditFamily` opens are batched behind the same progress bar since scanning every family in a large project library is the slow step.
- `family_health_config.json` alongside `script.py` (whether embedded-CAD scanning is enabled by default — it is the most expensive step — a size/instance-count threshold below which a finding isn't worth surfacing), mirroring `slab_convert_config.json`'s override-by-config convention.
- Sits in `Essential.panel/Bulk Tool.stack/`, own `bundle.yaml`/`README.md`/`CHANGELOG.md` per the root README's documented versioned-tool layout.

**Future update:**
- Auto-fix for duplicate-family clusters once `Type Consolidator`'s own reassign-then-delete pattern is proven safe in production: repoint every instance from a redundant family's matching type onto the canonical family's equivalent type, then delete the redundant family — the same escalation Type Consolidator's own future-update section already plans for a "near-miss" tier, one layer up.
- In-place-family "convert to loadable" assist: `SaveAsFamily`-export the in-place family's geometry to a new `.rfa` on disk and prompt the modeler to load it back in as a real family, turning the flag into a one-click remediation instead of only a report line.
- Per-finding recovered-byte estimate, using the temporary `EditFamily` document's own saved-`.rfa` size on disk, so the report ranks findings by actual file-size impact rather than a plain flag list.
- Fold the census into `Extract Template.pushbutton`'s existing report (`structural.inventory.gather()`) as an additional section, so a firm's template file gets the same health check applied to the standard it hands out to every new project, catching an in-place family or an embedded-CAD component before it is ever copied downstream.

## 2026-09-19 05:41 UTC

**Idea name:** Grid Sync — a direct pushbutton for the already-working GRIDS-sheet engine, so setting out (or resyncing) a structural grid stops requiring the bridge, an HTTP call, or an Excel macro

**Potential:**
Laying out a project's grid system is one of the very first things a structural modeler does and one of the most repeated after that — every bay-spacing revision means deleting or nudging a run of grids by hand, one at a time, and retyping bubble names to match. The tool to do this from a spreadsheet in one pass **already exists, fully built and tested**, and almost nobody who opens Revit can reach it. `anongee_toolkit.rc_automation.datum` carries the whole engine — `GridRow`/`GridPlan` (`datum.py:623,649`), `parse_grids()` reading a GRIDS sheet's Name/X1/Y1/X2/Y2/Angle columns via `_grid_header()` (`datum.py:673,730`), `grid_line()` resolving whichever columns a row actually filled in against a sheet-wide `default_length()` for angle-only rows (`datum.py:765,784`), and `plan_grids()` (`datum.py:855-961`) matching sheet rows to existing model grids by name-or-folded-name, refusing to move a grid whose direction disagrees (`"changing a grid's direction means recreating it, which loses anything dimensioned or constrained to it"`, `datum.py:918-925`) and refusing to create a second grid on a line already occupied (`_on_line`, `datum.py:963-970`). `anongee_toolkit/commands/grids.py` wires that straight to the API: `_apply()` opens one `TransactionGroup`/`Transaction`, calls `Grid.Create` for a new row and `ElementTransformUtils.MoveElement` for a matched one that only needs sliding onto the sheet's line (`grids.py:107-166`), and the whole thing is declared in the bridge registry as `"grids.sync"` with its own dry-run default (`registry.py:108-110`). The catch is entirely in how a modeler is meant to reach it: `commands.grids.run(uiapp, envelope)` only runs as a bridge job — the handler is bound by `commands.register_all()` inside `Bridge Listener.pushbutton`'s own `ensure_ready()` (`Bridge Listener.pushbutton/script.py:288-299`), which itself only happens once that pushbutton's window has been opened in the session, and even then a job has to arrive as a file in the outbox, written either by the HTTP route or, per `Bridge Check.pushbutton`'s own docstring, tested "from Excel instead" (`Bridge Check.pushbutton/script.py:1-9`) — i.e. from a macro a modeler has to have and know how to run. There is no equivalent to what levels got: `AutoLevel.pushbutton` gives levels a real interactive pushbutton, but it turns out to be a fully separate, independently-built engine (`anongee_autolevel/planner.py`, `textparse.py`, `naming.py`, `revit_ops.py`) that never imports `datum` at all — every "datum" hit inside it is the unrelated `DatumInput` elevation-offset field, confirmed by reading every match. So grids are worse off than levels: nothing in the repo lets a modeler point at a GRIDS workbook and press a button. The already-logged **Grid Mark Assistant** (2026-09-13) reads existing `DB.Grid` geometry to number columns and beams off it, but has nothing to say about creating the grids themselves — it explicitly starts from grids that already exist. This is the missing first step ahead of it. On the market, spreadsheet-driven grid setout exists only as one-off Dynamo graphs with no dry-run preview or direction-safety guard, or as a line item inside paid whole-project structural add-ins (Naviate Structure, BIM One's structural modules) sold per seat as part of a much larger bundle; nothing free turns a bay-spacing table into a checked, previewed, one-undo-step grid layout.

**Description:**
`Grid Sync.pushbutton` (new tool, `Essential.panel/StrucBIM.stack`, sibling to `Convert Slab.pushbutton` and `RoundElementDist.pushbutton`, the stack's two other structural-geometry tools):

1. **Pick a workbook** — a plain file-open dialog for the `.xlsx`, defaulting to the GRIDS sheet name `datum.SHEET_GRIDS` already expects, with a text field to override the sheet name for a workbook that names it differently.
2. **Parse + plan** — calls `datum.parse_grids()` then `datum.plan_grids()` against the model's current grids (the same `_existing()` read `commands/grids.py:87-104` already performs through `anongee_toolkit.structural.grids`), entirely off-Revit until the moment Apply is pressed.
3. **Preview checklist** — one row per sheet grid: name, resolved line, action (**create** / **move Δn mm** / **already matches** / **blocked — direction disagrees** / **blocked — line already claimed by "X"** / **invalid — duplicate name on the sheet**), reusing the same tick/untick-before-anything-happens shell `Convert Slab.pushbutton` and every other pre-flight tool in the repo already runs on. Blocked and invalid rows come pre-unticked, matching that shell's convention.
4. **Report only / Apply** — the same two-mode toggle `Convert Slab.pushbutton` offers; Apply calls the exact `_apply()` transaction logic `commands/grids.py` already ships (or that module directly, since it is plain, Revit-only code with no bridge dependency baked into the write path itself — only `run()`'s envelope-unwrapping wrapper is bridge-shaped).
5. **Result** — created / moved / already-matched / blocked counts, with the same per-row messages `plan_grids()` already generates (a grid the sheet doesn't mention is left alone and reported, never deleted), exportable through the `openpyxl` path `Export Schedule.pushbutton`/`BBS Generator.pushbutton` already use.

**Implementation:**
- Imports `anongee_toolkit.rc_automation.datum` and `anongee_toolkit.rc_automation.excel_engine.read_grid()` directly — the same modules `commands/grids.py` already imports (`grids.py:31-32,50`) — and reuses `commands.grids._apply()`/`_existing()` verbatim rather than re-implementing them a second time; the only new code is the file picker, the checklist UI and the plain (non-envelope) call into those two functions.
- No bridge, no `envelope`, no `Bridge Listener.pushbutton` session requirement — this button opens its own document handle and runs the transaction inline, on the click, the same way `Convert Slab.pushbutton` and `RoundElementDist.pushbutton` already do; `commands.grids.run()`'s envelope-unwrapping shell (`grids.py:28-84`) is not reused, since a modeler at a pushbutton already has the workbook path and dry-run toggle a UI control gives directly.
- Same modeless window shell (`anongee_toolkit.ui.hostwnd`/`checklist`/`progressbar`) every other multi-step tool in the repo runs on, and the same `openpyxl` report export `Export Schedule.pushbutton`/`BBS Generator.pushbutton` already prove.
- Ships with its own `bundle.yaml`/`README.md` per the root README's versioned-tool layout, in `Essential.panel/StrucBIM.stack/`.
- Worth doing the same for `levels.sync` right alongside this, since `AutoLevel.pushbutton` — confirmed above — does not actually cover that command's Excel-driven path either; a `Level Sync.pushbutton` built the same way would close both gaps with one shared UI pattern rather than one.

**Future update:**
- Extend the same picker to `footings.sync`/`columns.sync`/`beams.sync`/`slabs.sync`/`stairs.sync` (`registry.py:117-132`) once Grid Sync proves the "bridge command, no bridge required" pattern — each is exactly as bridge-only today as grids.sync is.
- "Since last run" diff mode, the same shape already logged for Type Matrix Generator, Sheet Issue Kit and the Analytical-Physical Alignment Auditor, so a resync after a spacing revision reports only what actually moved.
- Feed the resolved grid set straight into the already-logged Grid Mark Assistant as an optional next step in the same window, so setout and mark-numbering become one continuous run for a new project instead of two separate button presses.
- A saved-workbook-path-per-project setting, so a re-run after the first doesn't need the file picker again.

## 2026-09-19 00:41 UTC

**Idea name:** Workset Assigner & Hygiene Auditor — rule-based batch workset assignment by category/level/keyword, plus a whole-model audit for elements sitting on the wrong (or still-default) workset

**Potential:**
A repo-wide grep for `Workset`, `WorksetTable` and `WorksetKind` across every `script.py` and every `anongee_toolkit` module turns up exactly one hit outside third-party library code: `ConvertFloor.pushbutton/script.py:897`, inside `copy_instance_params()`, which copies `BuiltInParameter.ELEM_PARTITION_PARAM` (the workset parameter) from a source element to its recreated counterpart alongside `PHASE_CREATED`/`PHASE_DEMOLISHED` — a narrow, single-element "don't let a slab↔foundation recreate silently drop the element onto whatever workset happens to be active" preserve, not an assignment feature or an audit of any kind. The only other place the repo even reads worksharing state is `startup.py:298`, which logs `doc.IsWorkshared` into a startup diagnostics payload and does nothing with the value beyond that — it never gates a feature, never lists a workset, never touches `WorksetTable`. Nothing in the toolkit creates a workset, assigns one to a batch of elements, or reports which elements disagree with a firm's own workset standard. In practice this is one of the most common, slow-burning hygiene problems on any workshared structural project: a new element created while a modeler forgot to switch the active workset lands wherever that workset happened to be pointed — often the office-standard "Workset1" or someone else's discipline workset — and Revit gives no warning; the only native fix is switching the active workset, running Manage → Select All Instances per category, and re-checking the Properties palette workset by workset, a process nobody repeats often enough for it to actually hold, which is exactly why worksets on a mature project so often stop meaning anything (they exist to support partial-open scoping, borrow-performance separation, and discipline-level permissions, all of which degrade the moment elements are scattered across the wrong ones). A schedule with a Workset column bulk-edited in Excel and reloaded is the usual workaround, and it edits values with no rule behind them and no record of what changed or why. This is a distinct gap from two already-logged ideas that sit close to it: **View Graphics Sync** (2026-09-15 05:39) proposes propagating *view-level* workset visibility graphic overrides between views — a display setting, never touching which workset an element actually lives on; and **ConvertFloor**'s own copy is scoped to keeping one recreated element's workset unchanged during a conversion it already performs for other reasons, not to deciding what any element's workset *should* be. On the market, bulk workset assignment/audit tools exist only as one module inside large paid BIM-management suites sold per seat (CTC BIM Tools' Workset Manager, Ideate BIMLink, Avail) or as one-off Dynamo graphs shared without any reusable rule table or audit report; nothing free reads a firm's own category/level naming convention and both audits and fixes workset drift against it.

**Description:**
`Workset Assigner.pushbutton` (new tool, `Essential.panel/Bulk Tool.stack`, sibling to `Bulk Rename.pulldown`, `Bulk Delete.pulldown` and `Rebar.pulldown` — the stack that already owns "apply one rule across many elements at once" tools):

1. **Guard** — if `doc.IsWorkshared` is false (the same flag `startup.py:298` already reads, here made load-bearing instead of purely informational), the tool explains why and exits; no transaction risk on a non-workshared file.
2. **Rule table** — rows of `condition -> target workset`, where a condition is a category plus an optional refinement (level, a parameter value/contains-text check — e.g. "OST_StructuralColumns on Level 02 and above → WS-Structure-L02", "Family Name contains 'Rebar' → WS-Rebar"), evaluated top to bottom, first match wins. Rules load from / save to a per-project or per-office `workset_rules.json`, so one firm standard is written once and reused project to project instead of retyped.
3. **Audit pass (default, read-only)** — scans every element in scope (whole model / active view / selection, the same three-way picker `Convert Slab.pushbutton` already offers) and compares its current `ELEM_PARTITION_PARAM` against what its first matching rule says it should be. Flags: **wrong workset** (a rule matches and disagrees), **unmatched** (nothing in the rule table reaches it — commonly everything still sitting on the un-renamed default workset), **host mismatch** (a hosted instance's resolved target workset would disagree with its own host's workset — flagged rather than silently forced, since a shared/owner-controlled workset isn't always wrong to differ from). Nothing is written in this pass; it exists as its own standalone worksharing-hygiene report even before Apply is used.
4. **Preview checklist** (the same shell every pre-flight tool in the repo already runs) — one row per flagged element: category, current workset, target workset, matching rule name; rows tick/untick before anything changes.
5. **Apply** — one `Transaction`; creates any workset a rule names that doesn't exist yet (`Workset.Create`), reported before creation rather than appearing silently, then sets `ELEM_PARTITION_PARAM` per ticked row.
6. **Report** — assigned / already-correct-skipped / unmatched / host-mismatch-flagged / worksets-created counts, exportable through the same `openpyxl` path `Export Schedule.pushbutton`/`BBS Generator.pushbutton` already use, giving a BIM manager a paper trail for a worksharing audit.

**Implementation:**
- New `anongee_toolkit.revit.worksets` module — first in the repo to touch `Autodesk.Revit.DB.Workset` / `WorksetTable` / `WorksetKind` at all (confirmed by the zero-hit grep above): `list_worksets(doc)`, `find_or_create(doc, name)` wrapping `Workset.Create`, `current_workset(element)` reading `ELEM_PARTITION_PARAM`, `evaluate_rules(element, rules)` returning the first matching rule or `None`.
- Rule-condition matching (category + level/parameter refinement) reuses the same category-and-condition shape `MultiFilterParameter.pushbutton`'s filter engine already implements (Categories tab + operator/value condition rows), rather than inventing a second condition language — a rule row here is effectively a Filter-Parameter condition plus a target workset instead of a target parameter value.
- The `doc.IsWorkshared` gate reuses the exact check `startup.py:298` already performs for telemetry, now driving a feature instead of only being logged.
- `workset_rules.json` beside `script.py`, mirroring `slab_convert_config.json`'s and `tag_sweep_config.json`'s override-by-config convention already used across the repo.
- UI: the same checklist-with-per-row-status shell (`anongee_toolkit.ui.hostwnd`/`checklist`/`progressbar`) every other multi-step tool in the repo runs on.
- Sits in `Essential.panel/Bulk Tool.stack/`, own `bundle.yaml`/`README.md`/`CHANGELOG.md` per the root README's versioned-tool layout.

**Future update:**
- Level-range rule conditions resolved off the same sorted level table `AutoLevel.pushbutton` and the logged View Range Manager idea already build, so a rule can say "Level 05 and above" without hardcoding a level name that changes when a level is renamed.
- Optional auto-fix for host-mismatch rows once flag-only behaviour is proven safe on real projects.
- Hook into `BIMGeneration.pushbutton`/`RC Automation.pushbutton`'s own element-creation paths as an opt-in post-step, so newly created columns/beams/slabs land on the correct workset the moment they're created instead of needing a later sweep.
- "Since last run" diff mode — the same shape already logged for Sheet Issue Kit, Type Matrix Generator and the Analytical-Physical Alignment Auditor — so a re-audit reports only what drifted since the last pass instead of the whole model again.

## 2026-09-18 15:43 UTC

**Idea name:** Revision Cloud Sync — model-diff-driven native `RevisionCloud` placement that turns "what changed since the last issue" into drawn, tagged, code-native markup, instead of a modeler eyeballing a redline and sketching clouds by hand

**Potential:**
Every issued set that goes out a second time needs revision clouds around whatever actually changed, and today that is entirely manual and entirely memory-based: a modeler compares the new plan against the last issue (or just remembers "I moved that beam and added two footings"), picks Revit's native Detail → Revision Cloud tool, sketches a rough polygon by hand around each changed area, on every affected view, then checks the sheet's revision schedule to make sure the right revision number shows against the right sheet — repeated, from memory, every single issue, with no model-side confirmation that a cloud actually covers everything that moved. A repo-wide search for `RevisionCloud`, `GetAllRevisionIds`, `Revision.Create` and `SetAdditionalRevisionIds` across every `script.py` and every `anongee_toolkit` module returns zero hits anywhere in `AnonGee.tab` or the toolkit — nothing in this codebase has ever touched Revit's own `Revision`/`RevisionCloud` object model. What the repo does already have, and what makes this worth building now rather than from nothing, is a fully-working diff engine solving the identical shape of problem for a narrower population: `BBS Generator.pushbutton/core/revision_tracker.py` already saves a per-export JSON sidecar (`_sidecar_path`/`save_revision`, `revision_tracker.py:29-58`) keyed by bar identity, computes each `BarRecord`'s own change fingerprint via `_compute_hash()` — an md5 of a sorted-keys JSON payload of its own tracked fields (`bar_record.py:50-61`) — and on the next export loads the previous sidecar and classifies every record `new`/`changed`/`deleted`/`same` (`compare_revisions`, `revision_tracker.py:80-119`). That engine exists purely to colour rows green/yellow/red in an Excel bar-bending schedule; it has never been pointed at the model's own elements, never grouped a change by which view shows it, and never created a single Revit annotation object. This is distinct from the two closest logged ideas: Sheet Issue Kit (2026-09-15 15:40) proposes its own sidecar diff purely to skip re-exporting an unchanged sheet to PDF/DWG/NWC — it never draws a cloud, never touches `Revision`/`RevisionCloud`; Warning Auditor (2026-09-12 10:39) reads Revit's standing warning log, not a diff against a prior model state, and produces nothing on a sheet. On the market, revision-cloud creation itself is native to Revit (draw-by-hand only), and automated "what changed, cloud it" workflows are sold only as line items inside large paid production suites (CTC BIM Tools, BIM One) that offer manual cloud-from-selection helpers, or paid tools (Naviate/Ideate-class) that manage revision sequencing/numbering but not diff-driven cloud placement — nothing found diffs a model against a saved baseline and clusters the changed geometry into placed, tagged `RevisionCloud` elements automatically.

**Description:**
`Revision Cloud Sync.pushbutton` (new tool in `Essential.panel/Action.stack`, sibling to `AutoDIM.pushbutton` and `Export Schedule.pushbutton`) runs in one window:

1. **First run — capture a baseline.** Scope: whole model, active view, or a category checklist (Structural Columns, Framing, Walls, Floors, Foundations, Rebar). For every element in scope, computes a per-element fingerprint — a hash of a configurable parameter set plus its `BoundingBoxXYZ` min/max in mm — and writes it to a per-project sidecar `revision_cloud_log.json` keyed by `UniqueId`, mirroring `revision_tracker.save_revision`'s exact shape (`revision_tracker.py:29-58`) one level up, from bar records to any element.
2. **Later run — diff.** Reloads the sidecar and classifies every in-scope element `new` / `changed` (hash differs) / `deleted` / `same`, the same three-way classification `compare_revisions` already implements (`revision_tracker.py:80-119`).
3. **Group by affected view.** Every eligible plan/section/elevation from `anongee_toolkit.revit.views.get_eligible_views()` (`revit/views.py:36-56`) is checked for which changed elements it actually shows.
4. **Cluster into cloud regions per view.** Changed-element bounding boxes are projected onto the view's sketch plane using `AutoDIM.pushbutton`'s own `project_to_view_plane(pt, view)` (`script.py:57-60`), then merged into regions using `anongee_toolkit.revit.geometry.bounding_boxes_overlap` (`geometry.py:40-59`) with an enlarged, configurable padding tolerance.
5. **Preview checklist** — one row per (view, region): element count, categories, change types present, and the target `Revision` (from `doc.GetAllRevisionIds()`, or "create new revision" inline).
6. **Apply** — one `Transaction`: `RevisionCloud.Create(doc, view, revisionId, curveLoops)` per ticked region, with an optional revision tag at the region's centroid.
7. **Report + rebase** — result table exportable via the same `openpyxl` path `Export Schedule.pushbutton`/`BBS Generator.pushbutton` already use, and the sidecar is rewritten as the new baseline.

**Implementation:**
- New `anongee_toolkit.revit.revision_sync` module: `snapshot(doc, scope)` / `load_baseline()` / `diff(current, baseline)`, a direct generalization of `revision_tracker.save_revision`/`load_previous_revision`/`compare_revisions` (`BBS Generator.pushbutton/core/revision_tracker.py:29-119`) from `BarRecord`-specific fields to a configurable parameter list read through `anongee_toolkit.revit.parameters.get_parameter_value` (`revit/parameters.py:36-82`) plus a bounding box read converted to mm with `anongee_toolkit.revit.units.ft_to_mm` (`units.py:38-40`).
- Hash computation mirrors `bar_record.py:50-61`'s `_compute_hash()` shape exactly.
- View-plane projection and anchor geometry reuse `AutoDIM.pushbutton`'s existing `project_to_view_plane` and `bbox_center`/`element_frame` (`script.py:57-60, 70-150`).
- Region clustering reuses `anongee_toolkit.revit.geometry.bounding_boxes_overlap` (`geometry.py:40-59`) as the pairwise merge test inside a union-find pass.
- New Revit API surface this tool is the first to touch anywhere in the repo (confirmed by repo-wide grep): `Autodesk.Revit.DB.Revision` / `doc.GetAllRevisionIds()` / `Revision.Create(doc)` and `RevisionCloud.Create(doc, view, revisionId, curveLoops)`, plus `ViewSheet.GetAllRevisionIds()`.
- `revision_cloud_config.json` alongside `script.py` (hash fields per category, padding tolerance, deleted-element clouding window), mirroring `slab_convert_config.json`'s override-by-config shape.
- Sidecar `revision_cloud_log.json` follows the same convention `.bbs_rev` already establishes.
- Sits in `Essential.panel/Action.stack/`, own `bundle.yaml`/`README.md`/`CHANGELOG.md`.

**Future update:**
- Converge with Sheet Issue Kit's proposed sidecar diff once it lands, so export-skip and cloud placement share one "changed since last issue" fact.
- Auto-set each affected sheet's revision entry via `ViewSheet.SetAdditionalRevisionIds` once basic placement is proven.
- Fold `BBS Generator.pushbutton`'s own `.bbs_rev` bar-level diff in as a trackable category.
- Per-category padding/cluster tolerance as a config refinement once real projects prove the default tolerance right or wrong.

## 2026-09-18 10:42 UTC

**Idea name:** Column Reinforcement Placer — closes RC Automation's own long-open SCOPE-3/SCOPE-4 gap by finally placing the column main bars and ties it already computes, then continuing them storey to storey with a code-based lap splice and starter-bar projection instead of stopping flush at every pour

**Potential:**
RC Automation's own README states the boundary outright — *"What it will NOT do: Create columns. Their reinforcement is worked out but not yet placed. Lap or start column main bars. Bars stop at the pour; the report says so rather than guessing a lap length."* — and the tool's own `todo-list.md` names this as a tracked, unclosed defect, not a hypothetical: `CRIT-3` (`todo-list.md:98-110`) reads *"reading the run against its own report. Every run says 'Placing 6 footings and 5 columns' and every run creates zero columns and zero column bars,"* with `SCOPE-3 — create columns` (`todo-list.md:320-323`, *"the mains and ties that are already planned. Closes CRIT-3 properly"*) and `SCOPE-4 — laps and starter bars` (`todo-list.md:325-328`, *"explicitly out of scope... recorded here so the exclusion is a decision rather than an omission"*) both still `pending`. What makes this worth building now rather than filed as "someday" is how much of it is already sitting finished and unwired: `anongee_toolkit.rc_automation.rebar_spec` already has `plan_column_mains()` and `plan_column_ties()` (`rebar_spec.py:682-746`) — the tie-set function already builds the three-zone confined-end/middle/confined-end layout IS 13920, ACI 318 Ch.18 and EC8 all require, and the main-bar function's own docstring says exactly what's missing: *"Laps and starter bars are deliberately absent -- the plan says so, the report says so per column, and a bar that stops at the top of the pour is honest about being incomplete in a way a guessed lap length would not be."* `anongee_toolkit.structural.rebar_factory` already knows how to write a column bar once handed one — `_style_for()` (`rebar_factory.py:300-302`) already branches `ROLE_COLUMN_TIE` to `RebarStyle.StirrupTie`, and its own comments (`rebar_factory.py:520,537`) already describe "what column ties come through as" and "what column main bars come through as." The Excel side is equally finished: `rc_automation.models` already parses `COLUMN_TYPES`/`COLUMN_PLACEMENT`/`COLUMN_REBAR` into `ColumnType`, `ColumnPlacement` and `ColumnRebarRow` (`models.py:262,367,490`), with `column_type()`/`column_rebar_for()` lookups (`models.py:568-575`) mirroring the footing equivalents exactly, and `ColumnPlacement`'s own docstring already states *"There is no Height. The column runs from its base level to its top level... a height column would be a second source of truth that disagrees the moment a level moves"* — the multi-storey structure this idea needs is already how the sheet is modelled. Even column placement geometry exists: `anongee_toolkit.structural.columns` (387 lines — `find_symbol`, `ensure_symbol`, `create`, `placed`) is a complete, working module, yet a grep of `RC Automation.pushbutton/script.py`'s own import block (`script.py:127-131`, which imports `rebar_hosts`, `rebar_run`, `footings`, `structure_run`, `element_params`) never once imports it — confirmed by searching every "column" reference in that script.py, which only ever *counts* columns and checks host-eligibility (`script.py:306-307,1324-1332,1402,1436`), never creates or reinforces one. The one place the orchestration layer (`rebar_run.py`) touches column data at all is `resolve_bar_types()` (`rebar_run.py:199-218`), which reads `workbook.footing_rebar + workbook.column_rebar` together purely to resolve bar-type element ids — there is no `plan_columns()` or `place_column()` anywhere beside `plan_footings()`/`place_footing()` (`rebar_run.py:118-258`). So every input this idea needs — the math, the write primitives, the parsed schedule, even the FamilyInstance placement — already exists in the repo, fully built and individually correct, and the only thing missing is the orchestration wiring one already-complete module to the next, plus the one genuinely new piece: continuity across the joint. That last piece is also the one none of the 28 already-logged ideas cover — **Rebar Cage Copier** (2026-09-17) explicitly clones an already-modeler-built cage and deliberately avoids RC Automation's code-standard generation problem entirely; **Lap & Starter-Bar Continuity Auditor** (2026-09-14) only reads *already-placed* rebar to flag short laps, and since RC Automation places zero column bars today (`CRIT-3`), that auditor has nothing to audit for column reinforcement until something like this creates it. On the market, a schedule-driven column cage with real cross-storey lap/starter continuity exists only inside paid, seat-licensed rebar-detailing suites (AGACAD's Rebar for Revit, Naviate Structure, BIM One's structural modules) sold as one feature of a much larger bundle; nothing free reads an Excel reinforcement schedule and auto-continues column steel across a storey joint the way this would.

**Description:**
Lands inside `RC Automation.pushbutton` itself — the natural completion of `SCOPE-3`/`SCOPE-4`, using the exact same workbook, validation, modeless window and chunked-`TransactionGroup` write shell the footing path already runs on, rather than a second, duplicate pushbutton shell:

1. **Match or create the column instance** — for each `ColumnPlacement` row (`models.py:490-518`), find a placed column via the same key-parameter index pattern `rebar_hosts.index_by_key()` already builds for footings, now pointed at `rebar_hosts.COLUMN_CATEGORY` (`rebar_hosts.py:27`, defined and already unused for indexing); when none exists and the run is in "Create structure and reinforcement" mode, resolve the type via `columns.ensure_symbol()`/`find_symbol()` (`columns.py:40-215`) and place it with `columns.create()` (`columns.py:252-276`) between `base_level` and `top_level` — the one import RC Automation's script.py has never made.
2. **Plan one storey segment** — pull `COLUMN_REBAR` rows via `workbook.column_rebar_for(type_mark)` and hand them straight to the already-complete `rebar_spec.plan_column_mains()`/`plan_column_ties()` (`rebar_spec.py:682-746`) using the column's own base→top span — zero new reinforcement math, only the per-column orchestration `_plan_one_footing` (`rebar_run.py:133-185`) already models for pads.
3. **Continue across the joint (the new piece, closing SCOPE-4)** — group `ColumnPlacement` rows into vertical stacks by grid/XY position across consecutive levels (the same stacking `columns.placed(doc)`'s XY-and-level return already supports, and the identical approach the logged Lap & Starter-Bar Continuity Auditor's own Implementation section already names for its *read*-side grouping); where a stack continues from the storey below, the lower storey's mains are projected past their pour by a configurable, clearly-labelled lap length (a bar-diameter multiplier — a sanity threshold, not a certified code check, the same posture `rebar_spec`/`validation.py` already commit to elsewhere) and the upper storey's mains start that same distance below its own base, so the two segments overlap instead of both stopping flush at the slab; a stack with nothing above yet is left projecting as starter bars, named per column in the report — replacing today's silent, undocumented flush cutoff with an explicit, reviewable one.
4. **Preview and create** — a "Columns" tab beside the existing footing preview, same per-row status vocabulary (`Create`/`Skip`/`Has rebar`/`Invalid`) plus a new `Lap: 470mm into 02 2ND FLOOR LVL.` / `Starter: projects 470mm — no storey above yet` column, nothing written until confirmed, one `TransactionGroup` alongside the existing footing write.

**Implementation:**
- New `rebar_run.plan_columns(doc, workbook, key_parameter, replace)` / `_plan_one_column(...)`, structurally identical to `plan_footings`/`_plan_one_footing` (`rebar_run.py:118-185`) — same `HostPlan`, same `STATUS_*` constants — matched against `rebar_hosts.COLUMN_CATEGORY` instead of `FOOTING_CATEGORY`.
- New `rebar_run.place_column(...)`, identical in shape to `place_footing` (`rebar_run.py:221-258`), calling `rebar_spec.plan_column_mains`/`plan_column_ties` instead of `plan_footing`, then `rebar_factory.place_layer` (`rebar_factory.py:466`) — already role-aware via `_style_for`'s `ROLE_COLUMN_TIE` branch (`rebar_factory.py:300-302`), so it needs no change to accept column `BarSpec`s.
- Column creation wires the existing, currently-orphaned `structural.columns` module directly — no new placement code, only the missing `import`.
- New `anongee_toolkit.structural.column_continuity` module (the one genuinely new piece): `stack_columns(placements)` groups `ColumnPlacement` rows into vertical stacks; `lap_length_mm(diameter_mm, condition)` is one small, explicitly-labelled config table; `adjust_for_continuity(mains_by_storey, lap_length)` is a post-processing pass over `plan_column_mains`'s own output — shifting a continuing storey's start point down and the storey-below's projection up by the lap length — rather than a rewrite of that already-correct single-storey function.
- `column_lap_config.json` alongside `script.py` (lap multiplier per condition, stack-matching XY tolerance, an off switch to keep today's flush-cutoff behaviour for a team not ready to trust auto-laps), mirroring `slab_convert_config.json`'s override shape.
- Ships as a version bump of `RC Automation.pushbutton` itself, with a `CHANGELOG.md` entry naming the exact `CRIT-3`/`SCOPE-3`/`SCOPE-4` items it closes — reusing that tool's own shell rather than standing up a second, duplicate one, the same reuse-over-duplication the repo's own idea log already argues for (Shared Parameter GUID Doctor, logged 2026-09-18).

**Future update:**
- Feed newly-placed column cages straight into the next `BBS Generator.pushbutton` run (it already reads native `DB.Rebar` regardless of provenance) so a bar-bending schedule includes column steel immediately — the same handoff the logged Rebar Cage Copier idea already proposes for its own output.
- Once real column rebar with real laps exists, the logged Lap & Starter-Bar Continuity Auditor gains an actual population to check for the columns this tool *didn't* place (hand-modelled ones) — the two ideas become a matched create/verify pair, the same relationship Rebar Cover Auditor already has with RC Automation's own footing cover-write path.
- A smaller-upper-column reduction mode: when storey N's section is smaller than storey N-1's, drop main bars per code rule (e.g. alternate bars only) rather than continuing every bar — the one complication a flat "N bars in, N bars out" lap rule doesn't handle, and the one thing `plan_column_mains` sidesteps entirely today by only ever looking at one storey at a time.
- Extend `column_continuity`'s stacking to shear/RC wall vertical bars once proven on columns — the identical widening path already named by the logged Column Stack Auditor and Lap & Starter-Bar Continuity Auditor ideas for their own column-only v1s.

## 2026-09-18 05:39 UTC

**Idea name:** Shared Parameter GUID Doctor — whole-model audit and one-click repair for shared parameters that share a name but not a GUID, the exact failure mode six existing tools' own `BuiltInParameter → shared GUID → name` fallback chain is built to swallow silently

**Potential:**
A shared parameter is identified by its GUID, not its name — Revit only *displays* the name — but every tool a modeler actually touches (the parameter picker, a schedule, the Properties palette) shows only the name, so nothing in the UI ever reveals when a project has quietly ended up with two different "ID" or "Type Mark" definitions bound from two different `.txt` shared parameter files: one from the office master, one baked into a vendor family, a consultant's linked model, or last year's project template before the office file was revised. This is not a hypothetical — it is the precise risk `anongee_toolkit/structural/element_params.py` already documents about itself and only half-guards against. Its own module docstring (lines 17-19) states the danger outright: "creating a second one of the same name would give two different projects two different parameters that read the same on screen," and `_text_definition()` (line 245) guards exactly one path — reusing a definition already present *by name* inside the currently-open shared parameter file before creating a new one. But `bound_names()` (line 113), the function every mode of `RC Automation.pushbutton` calls before writing a single value (`script.py:468`, `_prepare_parameters`), keys its whole "is this parameter already here" answer off `known[definition.Name] = ...` (line 141) — pure name, no GUID comparison anywhere in the function. A project whose `ID` shared parameter on Structural Foundations was bound from a different file than the one `app.OpenSharedParameterFile()` currently points to reads back as "present" and RC Automation happily fills it — silently writing into a parameter that is not the one the office's own schedules are built against, which keep showing blank for exactly the elements this tool just touched. The same blind spot is load-bearing in the repo's single most-reused matching routine: `ConvertFloor.pushbutton`'s `find_target_param()` (`script.py:866-886`), cited as the shared convention by four other logged ideas (Type Matrix Generator, Convert Column⇄Wall, Schedule Round-Trip, Sleeve & Opening Coordinator) and used by `ConvertFloor.pushbutton` itself for every slab↔foundation conversion, tries `BuiltInParameter` first, `new_el.get_Parameter(sp.GUID)` second — and when that GUID lookup returns nothing (exactly the mismatch case, since the target category's binding carries a *different* GUID for the same name) falls through to `new_el.LookupParameter(d.Name)` at line 883: a pure name match that looks identical in the run report to a clean GUID hit. Every tool built on that three-tier chain inherits the same silent failure, and nothing in the repo — a grep for `\.GUID\b` and `ParameterBindings` across every `script.py` and every `anongee_toolkit` module turns up matching and copying code only, never a comparison of *two* bindings' GUIDs against each other or against the office master file — has ever audited whether two same-named bindings actually agree. On a real multi-consultant structural job (a coordination model brought in mid-project, a rebar family bought from a vendor with its own embedded shared parameters, a merge of two teams' models after a script like Convert Column⇄Wall or Type Consolidator has been run on one side but not the other) this is the single most common cause of "the schedule is missing half its rows for no reason" support calls, and it is close to undiagnosable by hand — Revit's UI has no built-in GUID inspector; finding it today means exporting the project's parameters via a report add-in and eyeballing a spreadsheet, or trial-and-erroring a `LookupParameter` in the pyRevit console. On the market this exists only as one report deep inside paid whole-project QA/QC suites (Ideate BIMLink's parameter audit, Avail, BIM One's model-checker bundles) sold per seat as part of a much larger tool nobody buys for this one check; no free, standalone GUID-vs-name reconciliation tool exists.

**Description:**
`Shared Parameter GUID Doctor.pushbutton` (new tool, `Advance.panel`, sibling to `Parameter Combine.pushbutton` — the panel that already owns document-level parameter operations rather than per-element geometry ones):

1. **Collect every binding** — `doc.ParameterBindings.ForwardIterator()`, the same enumeration `element_params.bound_names()` already performs (`element_params.py:113-153`), but recording the full `Definition` per name instead of collapsing straight to a category set: name, GUID (when `IsShared`), the category set it reaches, and whether it is instance- or type-bound.
2. **Group by name, not GUID** — any name attached to more than one distinct GUID across the document's own bindings is a live collision; the same pass separately flags any name bound in the project whose GUID does **not** appear at all in the office's reference shared-parameter file (path picked once and remembered per-project, mirroring how `element_params.FILE_NAME`/`_file_path()` already resolve a shared-parameter-file location beside the model) — the "this binding came from somewhere other than our master file" case that produces zero visible symptom until a schedule silently stops matching.
3. **Impact scan per collision** — for each flagged name, `FilteredElementCollector` over the categories the binding reaches, counting how many placed elements currently hold a non-empty value under *each* competing GUID, so the report shows not just "ID is ambiguous" but "312 elements hold data under GUID A, 4 elements hold data under GUID B (added {date range} — likely from {family/link name if resolvable})."
4. **Preview & repair table** (same pre-flight-report-then-confirm shell every tool in the repo already uses) — one row per collision: parameter name, both GUIDs, element counts, and a per-row repair choice — *rebind to master GUID* (re-point the project binding to the office file's definition, matching `_shared_parameter_file()`'s existing "use the app's open file, else write one beside the model" resolution in `element_params.py:174-198`) or *migrate values* (copy every element's value from the minority GUID's parameter into the majority/master one via `TransferParameterValue`or a plain `Set`, then unbind the orphan) — never both silently; the modeler picks per row.
5. **Apply** in one `Transaction`, reporting rebind vs. migrate vs. skipped counts, plus — critically — a follow-up note naming every other tool in the repo whose own name-based matching (`element_params.bound_names`, `ConvertFloor`'s `find_target_param`) would have silently used the wrong GUID on this project until now, so a modeler knows to re-run anything that already touched the affected parameter.

**Implementation:**
- New `anongee_toolkit.revit.shared_params` module: `collect_bindings(doc)` (name → list of `(guid, categories, is_instance)`), `diagnose(bindings, master_file)` → collision list, `impact(doc, collision)` → per-GUID element counts, `rebind(doc, collision, target_guid)`, `migrate_values(doc, collision, target_guid)` — kept separate from `element_params.py` (which stays the "write our own identity fields" module) since this is a document-wide diagnostic, not scoped to RC Automation's five parameter names.
- Master-file resolution reuses `element_params._shared_parameter_file()`'s exact logic (`element_params.py:174-198`) rather than re-implementing "find or make the office file," so both modules agree on where the canonical definitions live.
- `find_target_param()`'s own three-tier order (`ConvertFloor.pushbutton/script.py:866-886`) gets one addition once this module exists: when the GUID tier misses but the name tier would hit, log the near-miss through `anongee_toolkit`'s shared logging rather than silently falling through — cheap to add once `shared_params.py` owns the concept of "these two bindings share a name but not a GUID," and it turns every future silent name-fallback in the repo into a visible, reportable event instead of a guess.
- Own `bundle.yaml`/README/CHANGELOG per the root README's per-tool versioned layout; read-only by default (steps 1-3 run with no transaction) so it is safe to run on any model, including ones this toolkit has never touched, as a first health check before any other batch tool runs.

**Future update:**
- Pre-flight hook: any tool in the repo that is about to create or fill a shared parameter (`RC Automation`, `BBS Generator`, a future `Type Matrix Generator`) calls `shared_params.diagnose()` for just its own parameter names first and warns inline rather than writing into a possibly-wrong GUID.
- Export the collision report to the same Excel hand-off shape `Export Schedule.pushbutton` already produces, for a BIM manager who wants the audit trail without opening Revit.
- Cross-model batch mode: point the tool at a folder of linked/coordination models (or every family in a project's loaded list) and reconcile GUIDs before they ever get bound into the host project, catching a vendor family's colliding shared parameter at import time instead of after months of data have piled up on the wrong GUID.
- Scheduled/on-open check: run the read-only diagnose pass automatically on model open (or on sync) and surface a non-blocking notification only when a *new* collision has appeared since the last run, so the tool polices drift without becoming one more manual step nobody remembers to click.

## 2026-09-18 00:39 UTC

**Idea name:** View Range Manager — batch view-range (Top/Cut Plane/Bottom/View Depth) calculation and apply across structural plan views, driven off the same level table AutoLevel already builds

**Potential:**
Every structural plan view Revit creates gets its view range from a fixed default template offset, not from the story it actually sits on — a foundation plan needs its cut plane and bottom set to actually catch footings and grade beams below finished floor level, a beam/framing plan needs the cut plane raised just above the beam soffit so beam outlines and the columns underneath both read correctly without the slab above bleeding through, and a slab/floor plan needs the cut plane set inside the slab thickness itself. Getting this right today means opening **View Properties → View Range** one view at a time, guessing offsets, and re-checking the view — repeated for every plan view on every level, and repeated again whenever a new level is inserted mid-project (a routine event this toolkit already treats as first-class: `AutoLevel.pushbutton` adds/renames/re-spaces levels from one modeless window). A repo-wide grep for `ViewRange`/`GetViewRange`/`SetViewRange`/`PlanViewRange` across every `script.py` and every `anongee_toolkit`/`anongee_autolevel` module returns **zero hits** — nothing in this codebase has ever read or written a view's range, even though `AutoLevel.pushbutton` is already in the business of creating the plan views that need it: `_apply_views()` (`AutoLevel.pushbutton/anongee_autolevel/revit_ops.py:473-498`) calls `ViewPlan.Create()` per new level and optionally assigns a `ViewTemplateId`, but never touches the view's range, so every level added through the tool's own "Auto Level: plan views" transaction ships with whatever default the view family type happens to carry — silently wrong for a foundation or framing plan far more often than not. Two already-logged ideas sit adjacent without covering this: **Grid & Level Extent Sync** (2026-09-16 15:40) batches the 2D *bubble* extents of grids/levels themselves, not what a plan view's cut plane shows; **Sheet Set Builder** (2026-09-14 10:39) batches *sheet* creation and viewport placement, and explicitly leaves the plan views' own settings out of scope. On the market, batch view-range setting shows up only as one line item buried inside large paid drafting-automation bundles (Ideate Sidekick's view tools, CTC BIM Tools' view range utilities) sold per seat; there is no free, standalone, level-aware view-range batch tool.

**Description:**
`View Range Manager.pushbutton` (new tool, `Essential.panel/StrucBIM.stack`, sibling to `Convert Slab.pushbutton` and `Draw Floor.pushbutton` — the panel that already owns level-and-plan-geometry tools):

1. **Scope** — collects every non-template `ViewPlan` in the project (or just the active view, or a pre-selected set from the Project Browser), grouped by its `GenLevel`, using the same "one row per level, sorted by elevation" shape `AutoLevel`'s `snapshot()` already returns (`revit_ops.py:59-84`: `elev_mm`, `plans` list per level).
2. **Classify each view** by its structural role — Foundation/Footing Plan, Framing/Beam Plan, Slab/Floor Plan, or "Other, leave as-is" — first guessed from the view's own name and view template (keyword match against config-defined patterns, e.g. `"foundation"`, `"framing"`, `"beam"`, `"slab"`), then editable per row before anything is computed.
3. **Compute a view range per row** from the level table: each role has a rule expressed as an offset from *this* level's elevation and the *level above/below* it in the sorted list pulled straight from AutoLevel's own level snapshot — e.g. Framing Plan: Cut Plane = this level + configurable beam-soffit offset, Top = next level up, Bottom/View Depth = this level; Foundation Plan: Cut Plane = this level, Bottom/View Depth = this level minus a configurable footing-depth allowance, using the *previous* level in the sorted list when one exists. Every offset is a named, overridable value in a config file, not a hard-coded constant.
4. **Preview table** (`anongee_toolkit.ui.hostwnd`/checklist shell, the same pattern `Convert Slab.pushbutton` and every other pre-flight tool in the repo already runs) — one row per view: view name, level, detected role (editable dropdown), and the four resolved values (Top/Cut Plane/Bottom/View Depth) shown against the view's *current* values so a change is visible before it's made; rows with an ambiguous/unmatched role are pre-unticked rather than guessed.
5. **Apply** — one `Transaction`, `PlanViewRange` built via `view.GetViewRange()` mutated with `SetLevelId`/`SetOffset` per plane and written back with `view.SetViewRange()`; one row's failure (e.g. an offset that would put Bottom below the model's lowest reference level) is caught and reported, not fatal to the batch.
6. **Report** — updated / skipped (role not matched) / failed (invalid range) counts.

**Implementation:**
- New `anongee_toolkit.revit.viewrange` module (first production caller of `GetViewRange`/`SetViewRange`/`PlanViewRange` in the repo, confirmed by the zero-hit grep above): `resolve_role(view, template_name)`, `compute_range(level_row, prev_level_row, next_level_row, role, rules)`, `apply_range(view, computed)`.
- Level ordering and elevation data pulled through the same shape `AutoLevel.pushbutton/anongee_autolevel/revit_ops.py:59-84` already produces (`levels` sorted by `elev_mm`, each with `id`/`name`/`elev_mm`) — either by importing that module directly if it's on `sys.path` for the extension, or by re-deriving the identical `FilteredElementCollector(doc).OfClass(Level)` + sort, so the "level above/below" lookup this tool needs matches AutoLevel's own notion of level order exactly rather than drifting from it.
- `view_range_rules.json` beside `script.py` — per-role offsets (foundation depth allowance, beam-soffit clearance, slab cut-plane fraction) and the name-match keyword list per role, mirroring `slab_convert_config.json`'s override-by-config shape every other configurable tool in the repo already uses.
- View-role auto-detection also checks `view.ViewTemplateId`'s name when the view's own name doesn't match a keyword, since many structural teams name plan templates consistently even when individual view names drift (`"L02 - Structural Framing Plan"` vs. a template literally called `"Framing Plan"`).
- Sits in `Essential.panel/StrucBIM.stack/`, own `bundle.yaml`/`README.md`/`CHANGELOG.md` per the root README's versioned-tool layout.

**Future update:**
- Hook straight into `AutoLevel.pushbutton`'s own `_apply_views()` (`revit_ops.py:473-498`) as an optional post-step, so a newly-inserted level's plan views get a correct view range in the same "Auto Level: plan views" transaction that creates them, instead of needing a second pass through this tool.
- Per-firm named rule presets (Foundation / Framing / Slab offset sets), saved and reused across projects, the same "saved profile" shape already proposed as a future item for Universal Renamer.
- Section and elevation view-depth batching as a second mode once the plan-view case is proven, for the far-side clipping teams also set by hand.
- Re-run/diff mode: report which views drifted from their computed range since the last run (a manual override, or a level re-space that AutoLevel itself performed) rather than silently recomputing over a modeler's intentional manual tweak.
